data-go=\"288\">Next →</button></nav></article>"},{"id":"test01","title":"The Value of an Unimpressive Reading","volume":"迁移阅读 第1组","anchor":"chapter-0289","paragraphs":["Suppose a research team is comparing two instruments that estimate the condition of an old metal surface. The newer instrument produces a clear number almost every time. The older one sometimes reports that the reading is uncertain. To a manager buying equipment, the difference may look decisive: a machine that supplies answers seems more useful than one that occasionally declines to do so. Yet this judgment leaves open a question about what the apparently successful answers represent.","The team tests both instruments on surfaces whose condition has been independently examined. On clean samples, the new instrument performs better. On samples covered by an unfamiliar coating, it still supplies confident numbers, but some are seriously wrong. The older instrument is less precise on the clean samples and more likely to flag the coated ones. Neither result makes one machine universally superior. The appropriate choice depends partly on whether a doubtful reading can be investigated before anyone acts on it.","The temptation is to summarize performance with a single average. This may be useful for comparing similar tasks, but the mix of tasks matters. If most test samples are clean, an instrument's weakness on coated surfaces can make little difference to its overall score. A customer whose work mainly involves coated surfaces could therefore buy the highest-scoring machine and receive the least suitable one. The summary has not necessarily been calculated incorrectly; it has answered a broader question than that customer needed to ask.","An uncertainty warning also has a cost. It may require another inspection, interrupt work, or leave a decision unresolved. Praising caution without considering these consequences would be as incomplete as praising a confident display without checking its accuracy. A sensible evaluation would compare the costs of additional investigation with the costs of an undetected mistake. It would also ask whether users understand the warning or simply learn to ignore it when schedules become demanding.","The lesson is not that clear numbers should be distrusted. It is that useful information includes a reliable account of where the number may cease to be useful. A purchasing report could show results for different surfaces, explain how warnings are generated, and state what action they require. Such a report might look less elegant than a ranking with one winner. It would give the person using the instrument a better chance of choosing for the work actually ahead, rather than for an average task that nobody encounters quite as described."],"questions":[{"type":"Main idea","stem":"The author compares the two instruments mainly to emphasize the need to","options":["give older instruments priority when purchasing new equipment.","choose the instrument that produces the clearest average result.","replace numerical reports with warnings about uncertain conditions.","evaluate performance in relation to actual tasks and the costs of mistakes."],"answer":3,"explanation":"全文从单一分数转向任务构成、误差后果和警告的实际用途，主旨是让评估服务于实际工作，而不是普遍偏向新旧某一种仪器。","evidence":[3,4,5],"shuffled":true},{"type":"Vocabulary","stem":"The word \"decisive\" Managers expect the system to reward skill while helping customers receive parcels sooner. At first, average delivery time falls. The improvement is real, but it does not reveal how the company obtained it. The software has no direct measure of difficulty. It treats the recorded time as a compact description of the worker's performance, even though the time also reflects circumstances the worker did not choose.","Consider two routes. One serves offices with reception desks; the other includes apartment buildings where recipients are often absent. A driver who regularly receives the first route develops a better speed record. The software then gives that driver more work resembling the jobs already completed quickly. Meanwhile, the driver on the second route becomes associated with delay. A difference in assignments gradually acquires the appearance of a stable difference in ability, and that appearance influences the next set of assignments.","Employees may respond rationally to the incentive. Some avoid tasks likely to damage their score, including helping a new colleague or waiting for a customer who needs extra time. Others enter incomplete information so that a difficult stop looks like an unsuccessful attempt rather than a slow success. Management may describe such behavior as resistance to efficiency. That description overlooks the possibility that the system has made the measured target more valuable to employees than parts of the service the target was intended to represent.","Abandoning the software would not automatically produce fairer schedules. Human dispatchers may have their own preferences, and an informal arrangement can conceal unequal treatment more effectively than a recorded one. A useful reform would preserve a traceable decision process while adding information about route difficulty and work omitted from the speed measure. Workers should also have a practical way to challenge an assignment history. An appeal that takes longer than the pay period it concerns offers little protection against an immediate loss.","The important question is therefore which decisions the system is competent to make from the information it receives. A speed record can help identify a problem worth examining. It should not silently become a complete judgment of the person attached to it. If managers use software to avoid explaining their decisions, automation merely gives old assumptions a more consistent voice. If they use it to make those assumptions visible and open to correction, the same technology can support a more useful discussion about what good work involves."],"questions":[{"type":"Main explanation and challenge."],"answer":3,"explanation":"全文说明速度指标会影响后续分配，并主张保留可追踪决策、补充难度信息和申诉机制；不是完全否定速度数据或取消管理。","evidence":[2,4,5],"shuffled":true},{"type":"Vocabulary","stem":"The word \"compact\" in A less obvious question is who will have to change their behavior in order to receive that service. The two questions are related, but a favorable answer to the first cannot settle the second. A longer opening schedule, for example, may be valuable even when the conditions attached to it deserve criticism.","Consider a hypothetical library that can remain open three evenings a week only because a technology company pays the extra wages. The company asks evening visitors to use a new digital card, which records the books they borrow and the time they leave. Officials describe registration as voluntary: anyone who objects can use the library during its ordinary hours. This description is accurate in a narrow sense. It tells us that nobody is formally required to register. It does not tell us whether the alternative is practicable for a worker whose shift ends after the ordinary closing time.","Supporters might point to a high registration rate as evidence that visitors approve of the arrangement. Yet the same result is compatible with another explanation. Some visitors may dislike the condition but value evening access more than they dislike it. Their decision expresses a preference between the choices actually available, rather than approval of every feature of the choice they make. To discover whether users would prefer a different arrangement, the library would need to offer or investigate that alternative. Repeating the registration figure would not supply the missing comparison.","This does not establish that the sponsorship should end. Closing the library earlier could hurt precisely the people whose choices are most restricted. Nor does it follow that every use of a digital card is intrusive. The relevant questions concern which information is collected, why it is needed, and whether a less demanding method could support the same service. A sponsor might reasonably require reliable accounts of expenditure without requiring a record of each person's reading. Treating these as a single condition would conceal an important distinction.","A sensible evaluation would therefore preserve two facts at once: the additional hours have a real value, and accepting those hours need not amount to endorsing the sponsor's entire proposal. The aim is to improve the terms on which useful help is provided. Declaring the gift either pure generosity or complete exploitation would make the debate simpler. It would not necessarily make the library better."],"questions":[{"type":"Main idea","stem":"The passage mainly seeks to","options":["evaluate a useful sponsorship by separating its benefits from the Restaurants using the name would have to follow an approved recipe. Supporters believe the rule will prevent a familiar dish from becoming a label attached to anything convenient. Critics worry that a standard will turn a living practice into a test that some of its longest-serving cooks cannot pass. Both concerns arise from the same difficulty: a food can be recognizable without having been made in exactly the same way.","The association searches old cookbooks for an authoritative version. It finds considerable variation. One writer used an ingredient that was cheap near the coast; another omitted it in a mountain town. A third recipe was prepared for a hotel rather than an ordinary household. Choosing the oldest printed example would settle which document had priority. It would not establish that the selected recipe was once the most common, nor that the people who never published a book were less responsible for the tradition.","The dispute changes when cooks are asked what they regard as essential. Some identify a particular flavor. Others emphasize a sequence of preparation or an occasion on which the soup is shared. Their answers do not yield one convenient formula, but they reveal that continuity can reside in more than ingredients. A daughter may preserve her mother's method while adjusting to what local shops supply. Calling the result inauthentic would require a definition of inheritance narrower than the one by which many families actually cook.","There are still reasons for a public standard. Customers should not be misled about where a product comes from, and small producers may need a shared description to resist imitation by much larger businesses. The choice need not lie between an inflexible recipe and an entirely unregulated name. A standard might specify origin and a few defining features while allowing declared variations. The difficult work would be deciding who participates in making that distinction, especially when some cooks possess practical authority without the time or money to attend formal meetings.","Protecting a tradition is thus partly a question about distributing the power to describe it. A recipe can preserve valuable knowledge; it can also make one group's version appear to be everybody's past. The association would do better to explain the purposes and limits of its rule than to claim it has finally discovered the soup's original identity. A useful border keeps a name meaningful without treating every movement within it as an invasion."],"questions":[{"type":"Main idea","stem":"The passage mainly discusses","options":["why customers The articles vary in style and appear under different writers' names. Agreement across several publications seems stronger than praise in one advertisement. It may be stronger, but only if the agreement has been produced by sufficiently independent work. The number of visible opinions tells the reader little about how many separate opportunities there have been to discover a problem with the product.","Imagine that all five writers received the same demonstration, the same photographs, and the same performance figures from the manufacturer. Two tested the appliance briefly; three rewrote the supplied material. None examined the condition under which its main weakness appears. The reviews may contain accurate statements, yet their agreement offers limited additional reassurance about that weakness. Counting them as five independent checks would mistake the circulation of information for the repeated examination of it.","This problem does not disappear when every writer acts honestly. A busy reviewer may judge the supplied figures plausible and regard a short trial as enough for a useful first impression. A website may clearly describe the article as an introduction rather than a full evaluation. Trouble arises when the reader cannot distinguish these purposes, or when a page designed for an initial announcement continues to look like a definitive recommendation months after better evidence becomes available. The lifetime of a page can be longer than the lifetime of its justification.","One response is to demand complete independence from manufacturers. That sounds attractive but may deny small publications access to expensive equipment. Another is to disclose who supplied the product and whether payment was involved. Such disclosure matters, although it does not tell the reader what was actually tested. A free sample can support rigorous criticism; a purchased item can receive a careless review. Financial connection and quality of examination are related questions that should not be collapsed into one convenient symbol of trustworthiness.","More informative reviews would describe their method, the conditions left untested, and the origin of important claims. A comparison service could then group articles that rely on the same source instead of allowing repetition to look like confirmation. Readers need not reject an early review simply because its information is incomplete. They should be able to recognize it as an early review. The aim is to make different degrees of knowledge visible, so that a confident conclusion does not acquire authority merely by appearing at several addresses."],"questions":[{"type":"Main The target is easy to communicate, simple to count, and well suited to photographs of volunteers holding young plants. It also measures only the beginning of a process. Whether the campaign produces shade, improves a street, or leaves residents with a row of dead trunks depends on work that is less visible on the day of planting. The apparent clarity of the target can conceal uncertainty about the result it is meant to encourage.","Suppose two districts receive the same number of trees. In one, a local team has money for watering and replacing damaged supports. In the other, those tasks are added to an already overstretched maintenance schedule. A year later, the planting records are equally impressive, but survival differs sharply. It would be misleading to explain the difference simply as a contrast in residents' enthusiasm. The relevant opportunity to care for the trees was distributed differently from the opportunity to appear in the campaign photographs.","Location matters as well. A tree planted where it can grow large may eventually benefit more people than several placed in unsuitable spaces. Yet a council judged only by the number planted has a reason to prefer inexpensive, easily available sites. This can leave the hottest walking routes without help while producing a larger total elsewhere. The number is not useless. It becomes inadequate when it is expected to stand for every benefit that a more complicated policy should provide.","Better measures would not necessarily be simpler. A program might record survival, future maintenance costs, and how new shade is distributed among streets. Each adds a judgment: how long should survival be tracked, and whose daily journey should receive priority? These are public choices rather than technical inconveniences to be removed from the report. Residents can contribute information about where relief is needed, but consultation should not be confused with an offer to transfer unpaid maintenance duties to the people requesting it.","A city could still announce an ambitious planting target. The promise would be more credible if it included resources for what happens afterward and a willingness to revise locations when evidence changes. A photograph captures a real achievement: someone has put a tree in the ground. It cannot show all the conditions under which that achievement becomes lasting public value. Following those conditions may produce fewer dramatic announcements. It gives the young trees, and the people expected to benefit from them, a better chance of reaching the future described in the campaign."],"questions":[{"type":"Main idea","stem":"The author mainly Reference</p><p><strong>In Paragraph 5, &quot;that achievement&quot; refers to</strong></p><div class=\"options\"><button type=\"button\" data-option=\"0\">A. securing lasting shade for every walking route.</button><button type=\"button\" data-option=\"1\">B. revising every unsuitable planting location.</button><button type=\"button\" data-option=\"2\">C. putting a tree into the ground.</button><button type=\"button\" data-option=\"3\">D. transferring maintenance to local residents.</button></div><div class=\"feedback\" hidden=\"hidden\"></div></div></section><nav class=\"chapter-nav\" aria-label=\"Chapter navigation\"><button data-go=\"292\">← Previous</button><button data-go=\"294\">Next →</button></nav></article>"},{"id":"test07","title":"The Quiet Expert at the Table","volume":"迁移阅读 第7组","anchor":"chapter-0295","paragraphs":["In a committee choosing a design, the person who speaks most confidently can appear to be the person who has examined the proposal most carefully. Confidence is useful information about what a speaker believes. It is less direct evidence of how much the speaker knows. The distinction becomes especially important when members possess different pieces of information and must discover not only which option seems attractive, but which questions have not yet reached the table.","Consider a group in which most members have read the same short summary. One member has also inspected a minor detail that could make the preferred design difficult to maintain. During discussion, the shared advantages are repeated because several people can confirm them. The maintenance concern is mentioned once, without much confidence, because its owner is unsure how serious it is. The meeting may end with growing agreement even though the most important uncertainty has received the least collective attention.","It is tempting to solve this problem by asking the quieter member to be more assertive. That may help, but it also places the burden on the person who already has to introduce unfamiliar information. A confident challenge can be socially costly when others believe a decision is nearly complete. Moreover, demanding certainty may encourage the member to exaggerate a concern merely to keep it in the discussion. The group then creates the performance it later treats as evidence of expertise.","A different procedure would ask every member, before hearing the others, to record one possible advantage, one possible weakness, and one unanswered question. The resulting notes would not tell the committee which design to choose. They would make it harder for a repeated familiar point to occupy all the available space. The group could then investigate an uncertain concern without requiring its first observer to defend it as a settled conclusion. Such a procedure changes what can be said safely, not just who is brave enough to say it.","Nor should silence automatically be interpreted as hidden wisdom. A quiet member may have little to add, just as a forceful one may be well informed. The purpose is to avoid making speaking style do the work of examination. A committee needs enough confidence to act and enough room for doubt to correct its direction. Those aims conflict less when members can contribute an incomplete observation without having to turn it into an impressive personal claim. Better decisions may begin with a less dramatic question: what do we need to know before confidence becomes deserved?"],"questions":[{"type":"Main idea","stem":"The passage primarily recommends that committees","options":["select quiet members because they usually know more than confident ones.","make room for examination instead of treating speaking style as expertise.","require unfamiliar concerns to be stated with greater certainty.","delay decisions until every uncertainty has been completely removed."],"answer":1,"explanation":"全文既不把沉默等同智慧，也不排斥行动，主张通过程序让信息与疑问受到检查，避免以表达姿态代替知识评估。","evidence":[1,4,5],"shuffled":true},{"type":"Vocabulary","stem":"The word \"assertive\" in Paragraph 3 most nearly means","options":["optimistic about the final choice.","forceful in expressing a view.","familiar with all the other members.","exact in recording a measurement."],"answer":1,"explanation":"要求安静成员more assertive，就是更有力地主张和表达自己；后文讨论这种表达的社交成本与夸大确定性的风险。","evidence":[3],"shuffled":true},{"type":"Inference","stem":"Why might growing agreement fail to resolve the group's main uncertainty?","options":["Familiar advantages may be repeated while unfamiliar information receives little attention.","The concern becomes important only after its observer states it confidently.","Every member has already read the same detailed maintenance inspection.","The advantages mentioned by several members must have ceased to be true."],"answer":0,"explanation":"第二段并不否定共享优点，而是指出重复确认可能占据讨论，使只有一人知道的维护问题缺少集体关注。","evidence":[2],"shuffled":true},{"type":"Attitude","stem":"The author's view of quiet members is that","options":["their uncertainty normally makes their observations unsuitable for discussion.","their silence is a reliable sign of expertise hidden from the majority.","their speaking style alone is not enough to establish how much they know.","their contributions should be accepted without further period."],"answer":1,"explanation":"预先写下优点、弱点与问题，为陌生且未定论的信息留出空间；它不自动决定方案，也不取消后续检查。","evidence":[4],"shuffled":true}],"wordCount":420,"html":"<article class=\"chapter\" id=\"chapter-0295\" class=\"vocab first\" href=\"#word-1\" data-words=\"1\">a</a> <a class=\"vocab first\" href=\"#word-1627\" data-words=\"1627\">committee</a> choosing <a class=\"vocab\" href=\"#word-1\"
